I'm a civil engineer, so I thought, 'Oh, I can manage this myself.' But dealing with the city zoning board was a nightmare. They kept pushing back on the setback requirements, and I remember thinking, 'Why is this so hard?' Anyway, the house is gone now, he kept it, and I'm in this apartment with terrible water pressure. But yeah, I just... I feel so weighed down all the time.

I don't know, I guess... I haven't really felt like doing much of anything lately. I used to love going to pub trivia on Thursday nights with my friends from the firm. We had this team name, "The Stress Analysers," which I thought was pretty clever since we're all structural engineers. But the last time I went, I couldn't even focus on the questions. The trivia host was this guy with a really loud, booming voice—it echoed off the exposed brick in this weird way. It reminded me of the acoustics in this old train station I worked on retrofitting back in 2018. That was a massive project. We had to reinforce the entire foundation without disrupting the historical facade. The masonry alone took weeks to assess because the original mortar was completely deteriorating. I spent so much time in that damp basement... kind of like my apartment now, honestly. But yeah, I haven't been to trivia in a while. I just don't see the point.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's been... I'd say it's been hanging over me for about a month now. It really started getting bad right around when all the financial mess from the divorce hit. We had to divide up the assets, and looking at all those spreadsheets just broke me. I used to love spreadsheets, honestly. When I was doing the cost estimation for the highway overpass expansion, I built this incredible Excel model. It had all these nested formulas to account for the fluctuating prices of steel rebar and the labor union's overtime rates. The union rep was this guy named Sal, who always wore these incredibly bright Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter. He said it helped him remember his honeymoon in Maui. Maui... I've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long, and I hate being stuck in a pressurized cabin for that many hours. The HVAC systems on commercial planes are actually fascinating from an engineering standpoint, how they cycle the air, but it dries out my sinuses terribly. But yeah... about a month now, just this heavy cloud since the money stuff started.

I've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long, and I hate being stuck in a pressurized cabin for that many hours. I haven't been paying attention to the scale or anything. I guess I'm eating normally? I mostly just order takeout because I can't stand the thought of standing in the kitchen to cook. I got Pad Thai a few days ago, but the delivery driver got completely lost. The GPS always routes people down this access road behind my apartment complex that the city blocked off two years ago. They were doing this massive overhaul of the municipal storm drains. The old corrugated steel pipes were completely corroded from the winter road salt. It’s actually a huge problem in urban hydrology, how much salt we use on the roads. It just eats right through the infrastructure. We had to design this specialized concrete culvert for a highway project once just to withstand the chemical degradation. The curing process for that type of concrete is so finicky, you have to monitor the temperature constantly or it develops micro-fissures before it even sets. Yeah, it's... it's exhausting. Just getting through a normal workday feels like I'm dragging wet concrete behind me. And even something simple, like taking a shower or just opening my laptop to look at an email... it takes more energy than I seem to have. It's like my battery is just completely drained. Speaking of batteries, I had to replace the one in my car last week. It just died on me right in the driveway. I had to call my neighbor to give me a jump, and he has this massive Ford F-150. It’s one of those newer models with the aluminum body panels. They switched to aluminum to save weight and improve fuel efficiency, which is a smart engineering move, but it makes the bodywork incredibly expensive to repair if you get into a fender bender. The tensile strength of that automotive-grade aluminum is impressive, though. We actually looked into using a similar alloy for a pedestrian railing system on a suspension bridge I worked on a few years ago. We needed something lightweight but durable because the wind sheer coming off the valley was intense. We ended up having to run all these wind tunnel simulations... but yeah, I'm just so tired. All the time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know if I've felt exactly like this before... maybe? It's honestly hard to remember. Memory is a funny thing, you know? It's kind of like how we store data for long-term structural monitoring. A few years ago, we were tracking the load-bearing stress on the old suspension bridge downtown, and we had to set up all these strain gauges along the main cables. They collect thousands of data points every single second. The server racks we used to store all that information in the site office got so incredibly hot, we had to hire a guy to install a dedicated cooling unit just for that one room. 

The HVAC technician who came out was this older man who kept talking about his prize-winning orchids. He said orchids need very specific ambient humidity levels to bloom, which actually made me think about how concrete needs the exact right moisture levels to cure properly without surface cracking. I tried growing an orchid once... someone gave it to me for my birthday, but I think I overwatered it and the roots just rotted out. It sat on my windowsill looking completely dead for months until I finally just threw the whole pot away. 

But... what were you asking? Oh, if I've felt this down before. I really can't say for sure. I just know it feels really heavy right now.

Well, yeah, it's been about a month now, I guess. It really started hanging over me right around the time I was dealing with all the financial mess after my divorce. Trying to untangle joint bank accounts and split assets is just... it's a nightmare. It reminds me of this project we did trying to retrofit a mid-century commercial building for a new tech startup. We had to separate the electrical grids for the different floors, and the original wiring diagrams were just completely useless. We kept finding live wires in the walls that weren't connected to any of the main breakers. It's a huge fire hazard. 

You know, most electrical fires in older buildings start because the insulation around the copper wiring degrades over time, especially if it's been exposed to temperature fluctuations. We ended up having to pull miles of new conduit through these tiny, cramped drop ceilings. The drywall dust gets absolutely everywhere. I remember coughing for days after doing a site walk-through, even with a mask on. Drywall dust is mostly gypsum, which isn't toxic, but it's an irritant. They actually use gypsum in agriculture to condition soil, especially if it has high clay content. It helps break up the compacted dirt so water can penetrate better... but anyway, yeah, it's been a solid month of feeling this way. Since the divorce stuff.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, before this past month? I don't... I don't think so. I really don't remember feeling this bad before. Usually, I'm pretty go-go-go, you know? Like, there's always a project, always a deadline. Especially in civil engineering, the deadlines are just brutal. If you miss a pour date for concrete, the whole schedule gets pushed back, and then you've got the general contractor screaming at you, and the client threatening to pull funding. 

Funding is always the biggest issue on municipal projects. We had this one city contract to redesign a stormwater drainage system, and the city council kept debating the budget for six months. Six months! By the time they finally approved it, the cost of PVC piping had gone up by like twenty percent because of some supply chain issue. Supply chains are so fragile these days. It’s crazy how a disruption in one factory overseas can delay a construction project in the Midwest. 

My brother works in logistics, actually. He drives one of those massive shipping container trucks from the port of Los Angeles. He says the traffic getting in and out of the port is just a nightmare. He’s always complaining about his lower back from sitting in the cab all day. I told him he needs to get one of those ergonomic seat cushions, but he never listens to me. 

But, um... no. To answer your question, no. I don't think I've ever had a two-week period where I felt this down before. This feels new. And awful.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm pretty sure I haven't. I really don't think so. I mean, my mood is usually fine, or at least I'm too busy to notice. Being a civil engineer means you're constantly putting out fires—metaphorically, of course. Though, funny enough, we actually had a literal fire at a site last year. Someone tossed a cigarette into a dumpster full of cardboard and dried-out lumber scraps. The whole thing went up in minutes. The fire department had to come out, and it completely ruined our poured foundation because they sprayed water everywhere and it messed up the curing process. 

Water damage is honestly one of the hardest things to mitigate in construction. Once moisture gets into the subflooring or behind the drywall, mold starts growing within 48 hours. Black mold, Stachybotrys, is incredibly toxic. We had to take this whole hazmat certification course just to know how to identify it safely. The instructor for the course was this guy who sounded exactly like a radio DJ. Like, he had that deep, smooth voice. He told us he actually used to do voiceover work for car commercials back in the nineties. You know the ones, where they talk about "zero percent APR financing" really fast at the end? 

I wonder how they train people to talk that fast without tripping over their words. I always stumble when I try to talk too quickly. My mom used to tell me to slow down and take a breath when I was a kid because I'd get so excited telling her about my day. 

But, right, the question. No, I don't think I've ever felt this down for two weeks before. Not like this past month.

It reminded me of the acoustics in this old train station I worked on retrofitting back in 2018. That was a massive project. We had to reinforce the entire foundation without disrupting the historical facade. The masonry alone took weeks to assess because the original mortar was completely deteriorating. I spent so much time in that damp basement... kind of like my apartment now, honestly. But yeah, I haven't been to trivia in a while. I just don't see the point.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling overly guilty or like you’re a failure at things, even when it’s not really your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I just feel like I've messed everything up. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer, a failed wife, just a complete burden to everyone around me. I lie awake and just replay every missed calculation or tense email like it's proof that I ruin things. Speaking of emails, I was trying to organize my inbox the other day because it was getting out of hand, and I found this old thread from when I was working on the pedestrian bridge project over by the river. We had to coordinate with the environmental protection agency because of some endangered freshwater mussels in the area. Mussels! Can you believe it? We had to completely alter the dredging schedule so we wouldn't disturb their breeding season. It ended up pushing the timeline back by three months, and the project manager was furious, but I mean, what were we supposed to do? You can't just pave over an endangered species. The water level was unusually high that year anyway, which would have made pouring the concrete footings a nightmare. But anyway, yeah, I just... I feel like a failure all the time. Like everything I touch just falls apart.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been... I'd say it's been hanging over me for about a month now. It really started getting bad right around when all the financial mess from the divorce hit. We had to divide up the assets, and looking at all those spreadsheets just broke me. I used to love spreadsheets, honestly. When I was doing the cost estimation for the highway overpass expansion, I built this incredible Excel model. It had all these nested formulas to account for the fluctuating prices of steel rebar and the labor union's overtime rates. The union rep was this guy named Sal, who always wore these incredibly bright Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter. He said it helped him remember his honeymoon in Maui. Maui... I've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long, and I hate being stuck in a pressurized cabin for that many hours. The HVAC systems on commercial planes are actually fascinating from an engineering standpoint, how they cycle the air, but it dries out my sinuses terribly. But yeah... about a month now, just this heavy cloud since the money stuff started.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don't really know... I haven't been paying attention to the scale or anything. I guess I'm eating normally? I mostly just order takeout because I can't stand the thought of standing in the kitchen to cook. I got Pad Thai a few days ago, but the delivery driver got completely lost. The GPS always routes people down this access road behind my apartment complex that the city blocked off two years ago. They were doing this massive overhaul of the municipal storm drains. The old corrugated steel pipes were completely corroded from the winter road salt. It’s actually a huge problem in urban hydrology, how much salt we use on the roads. It just eats right through the infrastructure. We had to design this specialized concrete culvert for a highway project once just to withstand the chemical degradation. The curing process for that type of concrete is so finicky, you have to monitor the temperature constantly or it develops micro-fissures before it even sets. I had to sit out there on site with a thermal gun for hours just staring at wet cement. But anyway... no, I haven't noticed any weight loss or anything, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even small tasks, like getting dressed or running errands, are taking much more effort than usual, or do you feel unusually tired most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's exhausting. Just getting through a normal workday feels like I'm dragging wet concrete behind me. And even something simple, like taking a shower or just opening my laptop to look at an email... it takes more energy than I seem to have. It's like my battery is just completely drained. Speaking of batteries, I had to replace the one in my car last week. It just died on me right in the driveway. I had to call my neighbor to give me a jump, and he has this massive Ford F-150. It’s one of those newer models with the aluminum body panels. They switched to aluminum to save weight and improve fuel efficiency, which is a smart engineering move, but it makes the bodywork incredibly expensive to repair if you get into a fender bender. The tensile strength of that automotive-grade aluminum is impressive, though. We actually looked into using a similar alloy for a pedestrian railing system on a suspension bridge I worked on a few years ago. We needed something lightweight but durable because the wind sheer coming off the valley was intense. We ended up having to run all these wind tunnel simulations... but yeah, I'm just so tired. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if I've felt exactly like this before... maybe? It's honestly hard to remember. Memory is a funny thing, you know? It's kind of like how we store data for long-term structural monitoring. A few years ago, we were tracking the load-bearing stress on the old suspension bridge downtown, and we had to set up all these strain gauges along the main cables. They collect thousands of data points every single second. The server racks we used to store all that information in the site office got so incredibly hot, we had to hire a guy to install a dedicated cooling unit just for that one room. \n\nThe HVAC technician who came out was this older man who kept talking about his prize-winning orchids. He said orchids need very specific ambient humidity levels to bloom, which actually made me think about how concrete needs the exact right moisture levels to cure properly without surface cracking. I tried growing an orchid once... someone gave it to me for my birthday, but I think I overwatered it and the roots just rotted out. It sat on my windowsill looking completely dead for months until I finally just threw the whole pot away. \n\nBut... what were you asking? Oh, if I've felt this down before. I really can't say for sure. I just know it feels really heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's been about a month now, I guess. It really started hanging over me right around the time I was dealing with all the financial mess after my divorce. Trying to untangle joint bank accounts and split assets is just... it's a nightmare. It reminds me of this project we did trying to retrofit a mid-century commercial building for a new tech startup. We had to separate the electrical grids for the different floors, and the original wiring diagrams were just completely useless. We kept finding live wires in the walls that weren't connected to any of the main breakers. It's a huge fire hazard. \n\nYou know, most electrical fires in older buildings start because the insulation around the copper wiring degrades over time, especially if it's been exposed to temperature fluctuations. We ended up having to pull miles of new conduit through these tiny, cramped drop ceilings. The drywall dust gets absolutely everywhere. I remember coughing for days after doing a site walk-through, even with a mask on. Drywall dust is mostly gypsum, which isn't toxic, but it's an irritant. They actually use gypsum in agriculture to condition soil, especially if it has high clay content. It helps break up the compacted dirt so water can penetrate better... but anyway, yeah, it's been a solid month of feeling this way. Since the divorce stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a major episode like this before—where you felt persistently down, lost interest in things, and had trouble functioning—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean, before this past month? I don't... I don't think so. I really don't remember feeling this bad before. Usually, I'm pretty go-go-go, you know? Like, there's always a project, always a deadline. Especially in civil engineering, the deadlines are just brutal. If you miss a pour date for concrete, the whole schedule gets pushed back, and then you've got the general contractor screaming at you, and the client threatening to pull funding. \n\nFunding is always the biggest issue on municipal projects. We had this one city contract to redesign a stormwater drainage system, and the city council kept debating the budget for six months. Six months! By the time they finally approved it, the cost of PVC piping had gone up by like twenty percent because of some supply chain issue. Supply chains are so fragile these days. It’s crazy how a disruption in one factory overseas can delay a construction project in the Midwest. \n\nMy brother works in logistics, actually. He drives one of those massive shipping container trucks from the port of Los Angeles. He says the traffic getting in and out of the port is just a nightmare. He’s always complaining about his lower back from sitting in the cab all day. I told him he needs to get one of those ergonomic seat cushions, but he never listens to me. \n\nBut, um... no. To answer your question, no. I don't think I've ever had a two-week period where I felt this down before. This feels new. And awful.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this past month where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I'm pretty sure I haven't. I really don't think so. I mean, my mood is usually fine, or at least I'm too busy to notice. Being a civil engineer means you're constantly putting out fires—metaphorically, of course. Though, funny enough, we actually had a literal fire at a site last year. Someone tossed a cigarette into a dumpster full of cardboard and dried-out lumber scraps. The whole thing went up in minutes. The fire department had to come out, and it completely ruined our poured foundation because they sprayed water everywhere and it messed up the curing process. \n\nWater damage is honestly one of the hardest things to mitigate in construction. Once moisture gets into the subflooring or behind the drywall, mold starts growing within 48 hours. Black mold, Stachybotrys, is incredibly toxic. We had to take this whole hazmat certification course just to know how to identify it safely. The instructor for the course was this guy who sounded exactly like a radio DJ. Like, he had that deep, smooth voice. He told us he actually used to do voiceover work for car commercials back in the nineties. You know the ones, where they talk about \"zero percent APR financing\" really fast at the end? \n\nI wonder how they train people to talk that fast without tripping over their words. I always stumble when I try to talk too quickly. My mom used to tell me to slow down and take a breath when I was a kid because I'd get so excited telling her about my day. \n\nBut, right, the question.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you've asked me this a few times, and I... I really did just come in because I can't shake this heavy, hopeless feeling right now. It's like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has just completely collapsed, and I'm just sitting under all the rubble. 

Rubble is actually incredibly difficult to clear. When we demolish a site, the concrete chunks have to be sorted from the rebar before they can be recycled. They use these massive electromagnets attached to excavators to pull the steel out. It's fascinating to watch, honestly. The magnet just hovers over the pile and all this twisted metal flies up and sticks to it. I saw a documentary once about how they use similar, but much stronger, electromagnets in scrapyards to lift entire cars. They crush the cars into these perfect little cubes. It always reminds me of that scene in Star Wars, you know, with the trash compactor? I watched the original trilogy so many times when I was a kid. My dad had them on VHS, the ones before they added all the CGI stuff. The tape for Empire Strikes Back was so worn out that the screen would get all fuzzy right when Darth Vader tells Luke he's his father. 

But yeah, to answer you... no. Before this past month, no. I haven't felt like this before. This feeling of just being crushed under everything is new.

I've always wanted to go to Hawaii, but the flight is just so long, and I hate being stuck in a pressurized cabin for that many hours. They were doing this massive overhaul of the municipal storm drains. The old corrugated steel pipes were completely corroded from the winter road salt. It’s actually a huge problem in urban hydrology, how much salt we use on the roads. It just eats right through the infrastructure. We had to design this specialized concrete culvert for a highway project once just to withstand the chemical degradation. The curing process for that type of concrete is so finicky, you have to monitor the temperature constantly or it develops micro-fissures before it even sets. I had to sit out there on site with a thermal gun for hours just staring at wet cement. But anyway... no, I haven't noticed any weight loss or anything, I don't think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even small tasks, like getting dressed or running errands, are taking much more effort than usual, or do you feel unusually tired most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's exhausting. Just getting through a normal workday feels like I'm dragging wet concrete behind me. And even something simple, like taking a shower or just opening my laptop to look at an email... it takes more energy than I seem to have. It's like my battery is just completely drained. Speaking of batteries, I had to replace the one in my car last week. It just died on me right in the driveway. I had to call my neighbor to give me a jump, and he has this massive Ford F-150. It’s one of those newer models with the aluminum body panels. They switched to aluminum to save weight and improve fuel efficiency, which is a smart engineering move, but it makes the bodywork incredibly expensive to repair if you get into a fender bender. The tensile strength of that automotive-grade aluminum is impressive, though. We actually looked into using a similar alloy for a pedestrian railing system on a suspension bridge I worked on a few years ago. We needed something lightweight but durable because the wind sheer coming off the valley was intense. We ended up having to run all these wind tunnel simulations... but yeah, I'm just so tired. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had episodes like this before, where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if I've felt exactly like this before... maybe? It's honestly hard to remember. Memory is a funny thing, you know? It's kind of like how we store data for long-term structural monitoring. A few years ago, we were tracking the load-bearing stress on the old suspension bridge downtown, and we had to set up all these strain gauges along the main cables. They collect thousands of data points every single second. The server racks we used to store all that information in the site office got so incredibly hot, we had to hire a guy to install a dedicated cooling unit just for that one room. \n\nThe HVAC technician who came out was this older man who kept talking about his prize-winning orchids. He said orchids need very specific ambient humidity levels to bloom, which actually made me think about how concrete needs the exact right moisture levels to cure properly without surface cracking. I tried growing an orchid once... someone gave it to me for my birthday, but I think I overwatered it and the roots just rotted out. It sat on my windowsill looking completely dead for months until I finally just threw the whole pot away. \n\nBut... what were you asking? Oh, if I've felt this down before. I really can't say for sure. I just know it feels really heavy right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's been about a month now, I guess. It really started hanging over me right around the time I was dealing with all the financial mess after my divorce. Trying to untangle joint bank accounts and split assets is just... it's a nightmare. It reminds me of this project we did trying to retrofit a mid-century commercial building for a new tech startup. We had to separate the electrical grids for the different floors, and the original wiring diagrams were just completely useless. We kept finding live wires in the walls that weren't connected to any of the main breakers. It's a huge fire hazard. \n\nYou know, most electrical fires in older buildings start because the insulation around the copper wiring degrades over time, especially if it's been exposed to temperature fluctuations. We ended up having to pull miles of new conduit through these tiny, cramped drop ceilings. The drywall dust gets absolutely everywhere. I remember coughing for days after doing a site walk-through, even with a mask on. Drywall dust is mostly gypsum, which isn't toxic, but it's an irritant. They actually use gypsum in agriculture to condition soil, especially if it has high clay content. It helps break up the compacted dirt so water can penetrate better... but anyway, yeah, it's been a solid month of feeling this way. Since the divorce stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a major episode like this before—where you felt persistently down, lost interest in things, and had trouble functioning—for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean, before this past month? I don't... I don't think so. I really don't remember feeling this bad before. Usually, I'm pretty go-go-go, you know? Like, there's always a project, always a deadline. If you miss a pour date for concrete, the whole schedule gets pushed back, and then you've got the general contractor screaming at you, and the client threatening to pull funding. \n\nFunding is always the biggest issue on municipal projects. It’s crazy how a disruption in one factory overseas can delay a construction project in the Midwest. \n\nMy brother works in logistics, actually. I know we keep going over this, and I'm sorry, I just... I really don't think I have. Like I said, I only came in today because I just can't shake this heavy, hopeless feeling I've had lately. It's like everything I tried so hard to build back up after my divorce has just completely collapsed, and I'm just sitting underneath all the rubble. 

You know, speaking of rubble, it's actually a huge environmental issue in our field right now. Construction and demolition waste makes up like, a massive percentage of landfills. We're trying to push for more sustainable practices, like crushing old concrete on-site and using it as base material for new roads. It saves so much money on transportation costs, too. Hauling heavy materials is incredibly expensive because of diesel prices. My uncle used to own a trucking company back in the nineties, before diesel got so crazy. He hauled produce, mostly. Mostly oranges from Florida up to New York. He said the smell of the oranges in the back of the trailer was amazing, but he could never eat an orange again after doing that for ten years. It just ruined them for him. Kind of like how working at a movie theater ruins popcorn for you. I worked at a theater in high school, and the smell of that artificial butter... ugh, it gets into your hair, your clothes, everything. 

But, um, anyway. No. Before this past month, I really haven't had a two-week stretch where I felt this down. This is definitely new for me.

I feel like we're going in circles, Doc. I mean, I really don't think I've ever felt like this before. Like I said, I only made this appointment because I just... I can't shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It's just sitting on my chest.
